INTRODUCTION.

The present volume contains a translation of the
S!r6zahs and Yajts, and of the Nyiyis. This part of
the Avesta treats chiefly of the mythical and legendary
lore of Zoroastrianism.

For a satisfactory translation of these texts, the etymo-
logical and comparative method is generally considered as
the best or as the only possible one, on account of the
entire absence of any traditional interpretation. I have
tried, however, to reduce the sphere of etymological guess-
work to its narrowest limits, with the help of different
Pahlavi, Persian, and Sanskrit translations, which are as
yet unpublished, and have been neglected by former trans-
lators. I found such translations for the Sirdzahs, for
Ytists I, VI, VII, XI, XXIII, XXIV, and for the Ny&yis^
(besides the already published translations of Yarts XXI
and XXII).

Of the remaining Yarts, which are mostly of an epical
character, there is no direct translation available; but a
close comparison of the legends in Firdausi's Shih N&mah
seems to throw some light, even as regards philological
points, on not a few obscure and important passages.
This has enabled me, I believe, to restore a few m 3 d;hs to
their original form, and to frame a more correct idea of
others^.

In this volume, as in the preceding one, I have to thank
Mr. West for his kind assistance in making my translation
more readable, as well as for valuable hints in the inter-
pretation of several passages.

JAMES DARMESTETER.

Paris,

YA5TS AND SIROZAHS.

YA5TS AND StR6ZAHS.

The word y ajt, in Zend ySjti, means properly * the act of wor-
shipping/ the performance of the yasna; and it is often used
in Farsi tradition as synonymous with yasna. But it has also
been particularly applied to a certain number of writings in which
the several Izeds are praised and magnified. These writings are
generally of a higher poetical and epical character than the rest of
the Avesta^ and are most valuable records of the old mythology
and historical legends of Iran.

The Parsis believe that formerly every Amshaspand and every
Ized had his particular Yart, but we now possess only twenly
Yarts and fragments of another ^ The writings known as Yart
fragments, the Afrln Zartort, and Vtrtisp Yart (printed as Yajrts
XXI, XXII, XXin, XXIY in Westergaard's edition), are not proper
Yarts, and have no liturgical character; they are not devoted to the
praise of any Ized.

The order in which the Yarts have been arranged by the Parsis
follows exactly the order of the Strdzab, which is the proper in-*
troduction to the Yarts.

sIrOzah.

Sirdzah means * thirty days:’ it is the name of a prayer com-
posed of thirty invocations addressed to the several Izeds who
preside over the thirty days of the month.

There are two Sirdzah^ but the only difference between them is
that the formulas in the former are shorter and there is also,
occasionally, some difference in the epithets, which are fuller in
the latter.

^ The Bahman Ya^t (see Yt. 1 , §§ 24 and following),

* In the greater Sirdzah the names of the gods invoked are
introduced with the word yazamaidd, *we sacrifice to;’ in the
lesser Sirdzah there is no introductory word, the word khshnao-
thra, * propitiation,’ being understood, as can be seen from the
introductory formulas to the several Yaxts.

VASTS AKD stRdZAHS.

In India the Strdzah is recited in honour of the dead, on the
thirtieth day after the death, on the thirtieth day of the sixth
month, on the thirtieth day of the twelfth month, and then every
year on the thirtieth day from the anniversary day (Anquetil, Zend-
Avesta, II, 315).

The correspondence between the formulas of the Sfr6zah and
the Yarts is as follows :

Zemyad Yart (XIX).

The Yajts that have been lost are, therefore, those of Khsha-
thra-vairya, Spe/fta-Armaiti, AmeretS/, Atar, V&ta, Asman, MSthra-
Spe»ta, and Anagbra rao^ioru. The second Yart, or Yart of the
seven Amshaspands, appears to have been no independent Ysurt ;
it was common to all the seven Yarts devoted to the several

sIr6zah I.

3

Amshaspands, and, accordingly, it is recited on the first seven days
of the month. One might suppose that it was onginally a part of the
Oimazd Ya^t, as the Amesha-Spe^^tas are invoked in company with
Ahura Mazda (SJrdzah i, 8, 15, 23). There may, indeed, have been
several Ya^ts for one and the same formula of the Sfrdzah, as in
all of these formulas more than one Ized are invoked : this would
apply not only to the Ya^t of the seven Amshaspands, but also to
the Vanaw/ Yajt (Yajt XX), which, in that case, ought to follow
the Tfr Yart (see Sfrdzah 13),

Not every Yart, however, is devoted to the Ized whose name
it bears : thus the Ardibehert Ya^t is mostly devoted to Aiiyaman ;
the Rim-Y gut and the Zemy&d-Yart are devoted to Vayu and to the
A^arend : but Aiiyaman, Vayu, and the ^zrarend are invoked in
the same Sfrdzah formulas as Ardibehert, Rfim, and Zemyfid, and
a Yart is named from the opening name in the correspondent Sfrdzah
formula^

The systematic order so apparent in the Sfrdzah pervades the
rest of the liturgy to a great extent: the enumeration of Izeds
in Yasna XVII, 12-42 (XVI, 3-6) follows exactly the order of
the Sfrdzah, except that it gives only the first name of each
formula; and the question may be raised whether this passage in
the Yasna is taken from the Sfrdzah, or whether the Sfrdzah
is developed from the Yasna.

The very idea of the Sfidzah, that is to say the attribution
of each of the thirty days of the month to certain gods, seems
to have been borrowed from the Semites: the tablets found in
the libraiy of Assurbanipal contain an Assyrian Sfrdzah, that is,
a complete list of the Assyrian gods that preside over the thirty
days of the months

L ORMAZD YA^.

The Onnazd Yajt, properly so called, ends with § 23. The rest
of the Ysurt, from § 24 to the end, is wanting in several manu-
scripts, and is supposed by the Farsis to be a fragment of the
Bahman Yart.

The Ormazd Yajt is exclusively devoted to an enumeration of
the names of Ahura and to a laudation of their virtues and eflScacy:
the recitation of these names is the best defence against all dangers.

§§ z-6. The names of Ahura Mazda are the most powerful
part of the Holy Word.

§§ 7-8. The twenty names of Ahura Mazda are enumerated.

§§ 9-1 1. Efficacy of these names.

§§ 12-15. Another list of names.

§§ 16-19. Efficacy of Ahura’s names.

§§ 20-23. Sundry formulas of invocation.

As may be seen from this summary, the subject has been treated
twice over, first in §§ i-ii, and then in §§ 12-19 J 7 ^^ if does not
appear that this Yart was formed out of two independent treatises,
and it is more likely that the vague and indefinite enumeration in
§§ 12-15, ^hich interrupts so clumsily the train of ideas, is due
either to an interpolation or simply to the literary deficiency of the
writer himself.

The Ormazd Yart is recited every day at the Hfivan Gfih, after
the morning prayer (Anquetil, Zend-Avesta, II, 143) : it is well
also to recite it when going to sleep and when changing one’s
residence (§ 17).

Speculations on the mystical powers of God's names have always
been common among Orientals. The number of these names
went on increasing: Dastfir N6shtrvftn wrote on the 10 r names of
God; Dastfir Marzbfin on his 125 names. With the Musulmans,
Allah had 1001 names. On the names of God among the Jews,
see Zeitschrift der Deutschen Morgenlfindischen Gesellschaft,
XXXV, pp. 162, 632.

We have three native translations of this Ya^ ; one in Pahlavi
(East India Office, XII, 39, and St Petersburg, XCIX, 39 ; edited
by Carl Salemann), one in Persian (East India Office, XXII, 43),
and one in Sanslidt (Paris, fonds Bumouf, V, 66 ) ; the last two
edited in our £tudes Iraniennes, II, 255).

22

YASrS AND stRdZAHS.

The second part of the Yajt, the so-called Bahman Yajt frag-
ment, is in a state of the utmost corruption. It is difficult to trace
any connection in the ideas, yet §§28, 29, 30 seem to point rather
clearly to the final struggle between Ormazd and Ahriman and to
the annihilation of the Daevas, and, thereby, some connection is
established between this fragment and the Pahlavi Bahman Ya.ft^
which deals with the same subject. If that correspondence be real,
§ 26 might refer to the beginnmg of the Pahlavi Bahman Yart, in
which Zarathu^tra is shown by Ahura the times to come and the
end of the world.

Of this fragment we have only a bad Pahlavi translation in the
St. Petersburg manuscript mentioned above.

III. ARDIBEHI^T^ YA6'T*

This Yajt is for a great part devoted to the praise of the Air-
yaman prayer, which is described as driving away all the diseases
and plagues that have been brought upon the world by Angra
Mainyu; and when the writer passes from the glorification of
Airyaman to that of Asha-Vahirta, which is put into the mouth
of Angra Mainyu himself {§§ 13 seq.), he makes him speak of Asha-
Vahkta just in the same way, and ascribe him just the same powers,
as he himself has done with legard to Airyaman. This is owing
to the fact of Airyaman being invoked in company with Asha-
Yahlrta in the second formula of the Strdzah^

The powers ascribed to Asha-Vahwta have their origin in the
twofold nature of that Amesha-Spenta, who being, in his abstract
character, the impersonation of the highest element in Mazdeism,
Divine Order and Holiness®, and in his concrete character, the
genius who presides over the mightiest of physical elements, Fire*
is one of the most powerful and dreaded opponents of Angra
Mainyu®. On the other hand, Airyaman is the genius to whom
Ahura Mazda applied to heal the nine, and ninety, and nine hundred
and nine thousand diseases created by Angra Mainyu®.

This Yajt is recited every day at the Gfihs H&van, Rapithwin,
and Aiwisrfithrem (Anquetil).

o. May Ahura Mazda be rejoiced I . . . .

Ashem Vohfi : Holiness is the best of all good ....

thurtra, one who hates the Dalvas and obeys the laws of Ahura;

For sacnfice, prayer, propitiation, and glorification unto [Hfivani],
the holy and master of holiness^ ....

IV. KHORDAD YA6T.

Only the first two sections of this Ya^ refer to its nominal
object, Haurvat^, the Genius of Health and Waters (Vend. Introd.
IV, 7, 33). The rest of the Yart refers to the performance of the
Bare^nfim ceremony as being the test of the true Zoroastrian.
As the Bareshnfim purification was performed by Aityaman to
drive away the myriads of diseases created by Angra Mainyu^, its
laudation is not quite unaptly inserted in a Ya^t devoted to the
Genius of Health.

The KhordSd Yost can be recited at any time. It is better to
recite it during the Gdh U^ahin, on the day Khord&d (Anquetil).

The text is coriupt.

V. AbAN YAiT.

The Abfin Ya^t (or YaJt of the Waters) is devoted to the great
goddess of the waters, the celebrated Ardvi Siira An&hita, the
*Apairi$ of the Greeks. Ardvi Sfira Andhita (' the high, powerful,
undefiled’) is the heavenly spring from which all waters on the earth
flow down; her fountains are on the top of the mythical mountain,
the Hukairya, in the star region. Her descent from the heavens
is described in §§ 85 seq.; it reminds one of the Indian legend of
the celestial Gangd.

This Yart contains much valuable infoimation about the his-
torical legends of Iran, as it enumerates the several heroes who
worshipped Ardvi Sflra and asked for her help. First of all is
Ahura himself {§ 16) ; then came Haoshyangha {§ 21), Yima (§ 25),
Asi Dahdka(§ 29), Thradtaona (§ 33), Keresdspa (§ 37), Fran-
ghrasyan (§ 41), Kava Usa (§ 45), Husravah (§ 49), Tusa (§ 53),
VaSsaka's sons (§ 57), Vafra Navdza (§ 61), (rdmdspa (§ 68),
Ashavazdah, the son of Fourudhdkh^i, and Ashavazdah and Thrita,
the sons of Sdyusdii (§ 72), Vistauru (§ 76), Y6lrta (§ 81); the
Hvfivas and the Naotaras (§ 98), Zarathurtra (§ 103), Kava Vi^-
tdspa (§ 107), Zairivairi (§ 112), Are^-aspa and Vandaremaini

(§ ”< 5 )-

This enumeration is interrupted by a description of the descent
of Ardvi Sfira from the heavens (§§ 85-89), and of certain rules for
her sacrifice given by herself to Zarathujtra (§§ 90-97). This in-
terruption may have been intentional, as it takes place just when

^ Who shall have sacrificed to Haurvatd/.

Aban yast.

53

the course of the enumeiation brings us to the times of Zarathujtia
and of the institution of the new religion.

The Yaft is opened with a laudation of the benefits bestowed by
Ardvi Sfira (§§ i-i6), and it closes with a description of her gar-
ments and apparel.

The first record of the worship of Ardvi Sfira is in a cuneiform
inscnption by Artaxerxes Mnemon (404-361), in which her name
is corrupted into Anahata. Artaxerxes Mnemon appears to have
been an eager promoter of her worship, as he is said ‘ to have first
erected the statues of Venus-Anfihita ('A^poWnyff, ^Avambos) in
Babylon, Suza, and Ecbatana, and to have taught her worship to
the Persians, the Bactrians, and the people of Damas and Sardes ’
(Clemens Alexandrinus, Protrept g, on the authority of Berosus ;
about 260 B c.). My friend M. Hal^vy suggests to me that the
detailed and circumstantial description of Anfihita’s appearance and
costume (in §§ 126-131) shows that the \^Titer must have described
her from a consecrated type of statuary.

The principal data of the Greek writers on Anfihita will be
found in Windischmann*s Essay (Die persische Anahita oder
Anai'tis, i8g6). One must be cautious in the use of the Greek
sources, as the Greeks, with the eclectic turn of their mind, were
inclined to confound under the name of Anfihita all the great
female deities of Asia Minor, and her name became a common
appellation for the Aphrodites as well as for the Artemides of the
East.

^ After his brother Takhma Urupa, who reigned before him, had

* When Yima began to sin and lost thejffbaxend (Glory), he was

Ajsi DahSka, literally ‘ the fiendish snake,' was first a mythical
personage ; he was the ' snake ’ of the storm-cloud, and a counter-
part of the Vedic A hi or Vrttra. He appears still in that
character in Yart XIX seq., where he is described struggling
for the jST&arend against Atar (Fire), in the sea Vourukasha
(Vendtdfid, Introd. IV, 38; cf. this Yart, § 90). His struggle
with Yima KhshaSta bore at first the same mythological character,
'the shining Yima' being originally, like the Vedic Yama, a solar
hero : when Yima was turned into an earthly king, Asi undeiwent
the same fate. In the ShSh N^ah he is described as a man
with two snakes springing from his shoulders : they grew there
through a kiss of Ahriman's. For the myths referring to Asi, see
Ormazd et Ahriman, §§ 91-95.

* Babylon (cf. Yt. XV, 1 9) . The usurper Aai, being a non- Aryan,
was identified with the hereditary foe, the Chaldaeans : the name of
Babylon united in it, at the same time, a dim historical record of
the old Assyrian oppression, then shaken off and forgotten, and
an actual expression of the national antipathy of the Iranians
for their Semitic neighbours in Chaldsea. After the conquest of
Persia by the Musulmans, Asi was turned at last into an Arab.
The original seat of the Asi myths was on the southern coast
of the Caspian Sea (£tudes Iraniennes, II, 210).

^ A Farsi poem, of a very late date, gives further details about
Gasdarewa. It was a monster who lived ‘ in the sea, on the moun-
tam, and in the valley,*’ he was called Fishnah zarab, because the
sea did not go above his heel (a misinterpretation of his Avestean
epithet zairi pSshna, golden-heeled, the Zend zairi bemg mis-
taken for the Fersian zarah sea) ; his head would rise to the
sun and rub the sky ; he could swallow up twelve men at once.
Keresfispa fought hun for nine days and nine nights together ; he
drew him at last from the bottom of the sea and smashed his head
with his dub : when he fell on the ground, many countries were
spoiled by his fall (Spiegel, Die traditionnelle Literatur der Farsen,
p. 339, and West, Fahlavi Texts, II, pp. 369 seq.).

In the Vedic mythology the Gandharva is the keeper of Soma,
and is described now as a god, now as a fiend, according as he
is a heavenly Soma-priest or a jealous possessor who grudges it
to man. Vlhii. was the original form of the myth in Mazdeism
IS not dear. In the Shfih Nfimah he appears as the minister of
Azi DahSka. Cf. Yt. XV, 2 7 seq., and Ormazd et Ahriman, pp. 99,
note 5: 215, note i.

Persian (ibid. XXIV), and a Sanskrit translation (Fonds Bumouf V ;

YASTS AND stRdZAHS.

VIII. TIr YAiT.

Tirtiya is the leader of the stars against the planets, as stars
and planets belong, respectively, to the worlds of Ahnra Mazda
and Angra Mainyu (Vend. Introd. IV, 36 ; Bund. II, g seq.).

This Yaft is a description of the production of the rain through
the agency of the star Tutrya. It has to struggle against the DaSva
of Drought, Apaosha, is first overcome and conquers at last. This
seems to be a refacimento of the old storm myths, which have
been in so iar renewed as the role of the hero in the original myth
has been transferred to a star. It is to be noticed, however, that
Apaosha is not described as a planet.

Tirtrya is Sirius^. It presides over the first month of summer
(a I June-21 July). This Yart appears thus to have been written in
a part of Iran where the dog-days must have fallen in July, and the
rainy season began in the last days of July, unless the place of
Tirtrya in the calendar has been changed at some later period.

This Yajt is recited on the days of Tirtrya, Haurvatd/ (as the
Genius of Waters), Farvardfai (as the Fravashis are his allies in the
struggle ; § 34), and B^d (the wind ; § 32).

The struggle between Tijtiya and Apaosha is described in the
Bundahix (VII), but it has there a cosmological character : it has
not for its object the annual and regular return of the rains after
the dog-days, but the production of the seas and lakes in the first
ages of the world.

G6j, ' the cow/ Kar is a personation of the animal king-

dom which she maintains and protects. She is also called
DrvSspa and Gof ^riln : Drv^spa means ' she who keeps horses
in health/ and is nothing more than an epithet of Gd^ ; Gd jdrdn
(from the Zend Gens urvan) means ^the Soul of the Bull* (the
primeval Bull). Although urvan is a masculine noun in Zend,
jet Gdfdrdn is considered a female angel, as this name is only
a substitute for G 6 s.

G 6 s is the angel of the 14th day (Strdzah I, 14), and her Yart
is recited during the GSh Ujahin, on the days of Gdj, Bahman,
Mih, and Rim (the same days as those on which the Mih Ya^
is recited; see above, p. 88).

G 6 s is hardly described in this Yart (§§ 1-2) ; the greater part of
it being filled with the several prayers addressed to her by the
Iranian heroes, Haoshyangha (§ 3), Yima (§ 8), Thradtaona (§ 13),
Haoma(§ 17), Husravah (§ 21), Zarathu.rtra, and Vifti^a. Her
worshippers and their prayers to her are the same as in the case
of Ashi Vanguhi (see Yt. XVII).

X. MIHIR YA^.

This Yart, one of the longest of the Avesta and one of the most
interesting in a literary point of view, is not very instructive for
mythology. It consists of long descriptive pieces, sometimes
rather spirited, and of fervent prayers and invocations for mercy
or protection. Originally Mithra was the god of the heavenly light
(§§ 50 , 67, 104, 124 seq., 136 seq., &c.); and in that character

he knows the truth, as he sees everything ; he is therefore taken
as a witness of truth, he is the preserver of oaths and good faith
(§§ 2, 44 seq., 79 seq., 81 seq., &c.); he chastises those who break
their promises and he to hlithra, destroys their houses and smites
them in battle (§§ 17 seq., 28 seq., 35 seq., 47 seq., 99 seq., 105 seq.,
112 seq., 128 seq., &c.).

Particularly interesting are §§ 115-118, as giving a sketch of
moral hierarchy in Iran, and §§ 121— 122, as being perhaps the
source of the trials in the later Roman Mithriacism. Cf. Vend.
Tntrod. IV, 8 and Ormazd et Ahriman, §§ 59-61.

XI. sr6sh ya^ hAdh6kht.

There are two Yafts dedicated to Sraosha, the angel of divine wor-
ship ^ : one is a part of the Yasna (L VII [LVI]), and this, the other,
is called the Sr6sh Ya^t H£dh6kht. Whether it belonged to the so-
called H&dh6kht Nosk^ one of the twenty-one Nosks of which
the original Avesta was formed^, or whether it was recited in the
Hddhdkht sacrifice ^ a particular liturgy, is a matter on which we
have no sufficient information.

The two Ya^ts have a few developments in common (see §§ 8-
9, 10-13): the Hddhdkht is more liturgical, the Yasna Ya^t is
more descriptive, and has to a greater degree the poetical imagery
of a Yajt

The Srdsh Yaft Hidhdkht is recited every day, during any gih
except the Rapitvtn. A Pahlavi translation of this Yast is extant
(East India Office, XII, 102 ; Paris, Supplement Persan, XXXIII,
259 ; edited in £tudes Iraniennes, II), and Anquetil mentions
a Sanskrit translation.

XIL RASHN YA^T.

Rashnu Razi^ta, ‘the truest True/ is the Genius of Truth:
he is one of the three judges of the departed, with Mithra and
Sraosha: he holds the balance in which the deeds of men are
weighed after their death : ‘ he makes no unjust balance . • . . ,
neither for the pious nor yet the wicked, neither for lords nor yet
rulers ; as much as a hair's breadth he will not vary, and he shows
no favour^/ He is an offshoot either of Mithra, the God of Truth
and the avenger of lies, or of Ahura Mazda himself, the all-knowing
lord (§ 2 seq.).

This Vast seems to be an appeal made to Rashnu to come and
attend the performance of the var nirang or ordeal (see p. 170,
note 3), of which Rashnu, as the Genius of Truth, was the natural
witness and arbiter (cf. Vend. IV, 34-55 [i54-t56]). As a god of
Truth must know everything and be present everywhere, he is
called from whatever part of the world he may actually be in.
This brings about an enumeration of all the parts of the world,
from this earth (§§ 9-22) to the highest heaven (§ 37), passing
through the Albdrz (§§ 23-26), the star region (§§ 26-32), the moon
region (§ 33), and the sun region (§ 34; cf. p. 73, note^z).

This Yart is recited on the days of Rashn, Murd^d, Asht^d, and
Zemyid (the i8th, 7th, 26th, and 28th of the month).

XIII. FARVARDiN YASTT.

The Fravashi is the inner power in every being that maintains
it and makes it grow and subsist. Originally the Fravashis were
the same as the Fitrzs of the Hindus or the Manes of the Latins,
that IS to say, the everlastmg and deified souls of the dead (see
§§ 49-52} ; kut in course of time they gained a wider domain, and
not only men, but gods and even physical objects, like the sky and
the earth, &c. (§§ 85-86), had each a Fravashi (see Ormazd et
Ahiiman, §§ 111-113).

This Yart is to be divided into two parts. The former part
(§§ 1-84) is a glorification of the powers and attributes of the
Fravashis in general; the latter part (§§ 85-158) is an enumeration
of the Fravashis of the most celebrated heroes of Mazdeism, from
the first man, Gaya Maretan, down to the last, Saoshya»/.

This latter part is like a Homer's catalogue of Mazdeism.
The greatest part of the historical legends of Iran lies here con«
densed into a register of proper names. This enumeration is
divided into seven chapters :

The first (XXIV, §§ 85-95) contains the names of several gods,
of the first man, Gaya Maretan, the first law-giver, Zarathurtra, and
his first disciple, Maidhyd-maungha;

The second part (XXV, §§ 96-110) contains the names of the
disciples of Zarathurtra, most of them belonging to the epical
cyclus of Vljtfispa (Gurtfisp) ;

The third part (XXVI, §§ 111-117) is of uncertain character,
and no name contained in it is found in the epical legends ;

The fourth part (XXVII, §§ 118-128) seems to be devoted to
the heroes of the other Karshvares and to mythical beings, bom
or unborn (cf. §§ 121, 12a, 127, 128) ;

The fifth part (XXVIII, § 129) is devoted to Saoshyaw/ alone;

The sixth part (XXIX, §§ 130-138) is devoted to the heroes
before the time of Zarathurtra;

The seventh part (XXX, §§ 139-142) is devoted to the holy
women of Mazdeism from Hvdvi, Zarathiutra’s wife, down to
Srfita/-fedhri, Vanghu-fedhii, and EredaZ-fedhri, the future mothers
of his three unborn sons.

The second, third, and fourth enumerations all end with the

N 2

l8o VASTS AND siRdZAHS.

name of AstvaZ-ereta (that is to say, Saoshya/tZ), nhich shows that
they do not refer to successive generations, but to three inde-
pendent branches, which are each developed apart down to the
time of the Saviour.

^ The sixth and last Gdhambir (see Afrigdn G&hamb^r), or the last
ten days of the year (loth-aoth March), including the last five days
of the last month, SapendSimad, and die five complementaiy days.
These last ten days should be spent in deeds of charity, religious
banquets (gaszn), and ceremonies in memory of the dead. It was
also at the approach of the spring that the Romans and the
Athenians used to offer annual sacrifices to the dead ; the Romans
in Februaiy ‘qui tunc extremus anni mensis erat’ (Cicero, De
Legibus, II, 21), the Athenians on the third day of theMthesterion
feast (in the same month). The souls of the dead were supposed to
partaJke of the new life then beginning to circulate through nature,
that had also been dead during the long months of winter.

XIV. BAHRAM YAi'T.

Regarding Bahr^tm (Verethraghna), the Genius of Victory, see
Vend. Introd. V, 8.

This Yajt can be divided into four parts : —

I (§§ 1-28). An enumeration of the ten incarnations in which
Verethraghna appeared to Zarathiiftra (as a wind, § a ; as a bull,
§ 7 ; as a horse, § 9; as a camel, § ii ; as a boar, § 15 ; as a
youth, § 17 ; as a raven, § 19 ; as a ram, § 23 ; as a buck, § 25 ;
and as a man, § 27).

II (§§ 30-33)- The powers given by Verethraghna to his wor-
shipper, Zarathu^a.

III (§§ 34-46). The magical powers, ascribed to the raven’s
feather, of stnking tenor into an army and dispersing it (the raven
being the seventh incarnation of Verethraghna).

IV (§§ 47-64). The glorification of Verethraghna.

0. May Ahura Mazda be rejoiced 1 ....

thurtra, one who hates the Daevas and obeys the laws of Ahura ;

the holy and master of holiness ....

Unto Verethraghna, made by Mazda, and unto
the crushing Ascendant ' ;

cation.

XV. RAM YAiT.

This Ya^t bears the name of R^a the Genius who

presides over the 21st day of the month (Strdzah, § 21), and is
devoted to his Hamkir, Yz.y\x.

Regarding Rdma J?z;lftra, the Genius that gives good abodes
and good pastures, and his connection with Vayu, see Vend. Introd.
IV, and £tudes Iramennes, II, 187.

This Yaft can be divided into two parts. The first part (§§ 1-140)
contains an enumeration of worshippers who sacrificed to Vayu :
Ahura Mazda (§ 2), Haoshyangha (§ 7), Takhma Urupa (§11),
Yima (§ 15), Asi Dahfika (§ 19), ThraStaona (§ 23), Keres^pa
(§ 27), Aurvasixa (§ 31), Hutaosa (§ 35), and Iranian maids (§ 39),
The second part (§§ 42-58) contains a special enumeration and
glorification of the many names of Vayu (§§ 42-50).

XVI. DlN YA^.

Din (Da^na) presides over the 24th day of the month (Sfrdzah 24)
and gives it her name ; she is invoked in company with ^sta, and
in fact this Ya^t, though it bears the name of DaSna, is consecrated
to -ffista (§§ 2, 7). These two Genii are, however, very closely
connected in their nature, as Dalna is the impersonation of the
Zoroastrian Law or Religion, and j^sta is religious knowledge,
the knowledge of what leads to bliss (far^^ak, nirvft»a^^a ; the
same as j?isti).

The description of JSTista is rather pallid, and does not rise above
abstractions (see, however, Mihir Yajt, § 126). She was not wor-
shipped by the old epic heroes as Anihita was, but by Zarathurtra
and his wife, because she must have been, from her very name and
nature, a goddess of Zoroastrian origin and growth.

XVII. ASHI VAST.

Ashi Vanguhi or * the good Ashi ^ Ms a feminine impersonation
of piety ®, and she is, at the same time, the source of all the good
and riches that are connected with piety ^ She is described, there-
fore, as a goddess of Fortune and Wealth, and is invoked in com-
pany with Pire«di, the goddess of Treasures (Shdzah 25).

She appears in the latter character in the first part of the Vast

YASrS AND SIRdZAHS.

XIX. zamyAd vast .

This Yajt, inscribed to the Genius of the Earth, is devoted to
a description of the mountains and the kingly Glory (kavaSm
Zr»aren6), which are invoked, together with the Earth, in the
corresponding formula of the Sfrdzah (§ 28) : there is no Yajt
devoted to the Earth itself.

The mountains are simply enumerated (§§ 1-8). The rest of
the Yajt is devoted to the praise of the Z?»aren6, or, more pre-
cisely, to that of those who possessed it, whose powers or feats
are described. The list begins with Ahura Mazda (§ 10), and closes
with Saoshyaff/ (§ 89) ; that is to say, it begins with the beginning
of the world, and closes with its end It includes the Amesha-
Spewtas (§ 15), Haoshyangha (§ 26), Takhma Urupa (§ 28), Yima
(§ 31), Mithra (§ 33), ThraStaona (§ 36), Keresftspa (§ 38), the
kings of the Kaianyan dynasty (§§ 66-72), Kavi Husravah (§ 74),
Zarathurtra (§ 79), VfjtSspa (§ 84). The unsuccessful efforts of
Fianghrasyan to take possession of it are described at length
{§§ S6-64).

This Ya^t ould serve as a short history of the Iranian monarchy,
an abridged Shih Nimah.

XXI AND XXII. VAST FRAGMENTS.

These two Yaits or Ysut fragments are known among the Farsis
as the Hidhdkht Nask, though their context does not correspond
to any part of the descnption of that Nask as given in the Din-
kart (West, Pahlavi Texts, I, 224, note 8). A Pahlavi translation
of these Yarts has been edited by Haug and West (The Book of
ArdS Vitif, p. 269 seq.).

XXI. VAST FRAGMENT.

Yart XXI is a eulogy of the Ashem Yohii prayer, the value
of which rises higher and higher, according as the circumstances
under which it is being recited are of greater importance.

1. Zarathiwtra asked Ahura Mazda: *0 Ahura
Mazda, most beneficent Spirit, Maker of the mate-
rial world, thou Holy One !

‘ What is the only word in which is contained the
glorification of all good things, of all the things that
are the ofispring of the good principle ? '

2. Ahura Mazda answered: ‘It is the praise of
Holiness ^ O Spitama Zarathortra!

3. * He who recites the praise of Holiness ^ in the
fulness of faith and with a devoted heart, praises
me, Ahura Mazda ; he praises the waters, he praises
the earth, he praises the cattle, he praises the plants,
he praises all good things made by Mazda, all the
things that are the offspring of the good principle,

4. ‘For the reciting of that word of truth, O
Zarathurtra I the pronouncing of that formula, the
Ahuna Vairya, increases strength and victory in
one’s soul and piety.

YASTS AND stadZAHS.

YAST XXIL

This Yart is a description of the fate that attends the soul of the
t^hteous (§§ i-i8) and the soul of the wicked (§§ 19-3Y) after
death. They spend the first three nights (the sadis or sidds ; cf.
Commentaire du Vendidfid, XIII, 55) amongst the highest enjoy-
ments or pains ; they are then met by their own conscience m the
shape of a beautifiil heavenly maiden (or a fiendish old woman ^),
and are brought in four steps up to heaven or down to hell, through
the three paradises of Good-Thought, Good-Word, and Good-
Deed, or the three hells of Evil-Thought, Evil-Word, and Evil-
Deed : there they axe praised and glorified by Ahura, or rebuked
and insulted by Angra Mainyu, and fed with ambrosia or poison.

Similar developments are to be found in Yart XXIV, 53-65 ;
Ardfi Virfif XVII ; Minokhired II, 123-194.

I.

1. Zarathujtra asked Ahura Mazda: ‘O Ahura
Mazda, most beneficent Spirit, Maker of the material
world, thou Holy One!

‘ When one of the faithful departs this life, where
does his soul abide on that night ? ’

Ahura Mazda answered :

2. ‘It takes its seat near the head, singing the
U.ftavaiti Githa^ and proclaiming happiness:
“ Happy is he, happy the man, whoever he be, to
whom Ahura Mazda gives the full accomplishment
of his wishes I ” On that night his soul tastes * as
much of pleasure as the whole of the living world
can taste.’

‘God taught the Zendivasta to Zartusht— a sublime work . . . .
Gk)d said to Zartusht, “Go and before Shih Gushtdsp read this
book, that he may come into the faith .... keep all my counsel
and repeat it i;^ord by word to Shdh Gushtdsp V* ’

Zartusht, in obedience to God, went to the court of Gushtdsp: ‘He
came forward and called down a blessing on the Shdh*/ Then he
read to him the Zanddvasta and said : ‘Learn its statutes and walk
therein. If your desire is towards its laws, your abode shall be in
the paradise of heaven. But if you turn away from its command-
ments, you shall bring down your crowned head to the dust. Your
God will be displeased with you, and will overthrow your pros-
perous condition. At the last you shall descend into hell, if you
hear not the counsel of the Almighty*.*

These lines of the Zartusht-Namah are a summary of the following
two YaJts. The first, entitled ‘ The blessing of the prophet Zartfif t/
contains the words of blessing addressed by Zarathurtra when
appearing before the king. These words seem to have followed
a similar blessing pronounced by (rim&spa \ the prime minister of
Vijtflspa®.

Yart XXIV contains the exhortations of the prophet to the
long to follow and closdy adhere to the Law of Mazda. It is a
counterpart to the XIXth Fargard of the Vendfdld, as Zarathortra
plays here the same part to Vlrt^spa as Ahura does there to Zara-
thuftra. It is, therefore, a summary of the Law, of the duties it

enforces and of the rewards it promises. This accounts for the

For this translation I was able to avail myself of a Pahlavi trans-
lation, of which a copy was kindly lent to me by Mr. West.
That translation is apparendy of late date and often manifestly
wrong; yet it was very useful to me in several passages, besides its
giving a Zend text generally more correct and more correctly
divided than the text in Westergaard’s edition ^

Vast XXni was originally no independent Ya^t, being nothing
more than the beginning of Yast XXIV, detached from it, with
some slight alterations and inversions.

NYAYI5.

NYAYI5.

A Nydyij is properly a begging prayer, as opposed to SMyis,
a prayer of praise. It is a term partical^ly applied to five prayers
addressed to the Sun, to Mithra, to the Moon, to Waters, and to
Fire. Every layman over eight years old is bound to recite the
Ny&yir: he recites it standing and girded with his Kdstf.

The Sun Nyiylr is recited three times a day, at the rising of the
sun (Gdh Hdvan), at noon (Gib Rapitvin), and at three o’clock in
the afternoon (Gdh Uzlren). The Mithra Nydyir is recited with
the Sun Nyiyu, as Mithra follows the sun in its course (see
Yt.X, 13).

The Moon Nydyir is recited three times a month : first, at the
time when it begins to be seen; second, when it is at the full;
third, when it is on the wane.

The Waters Nyfiyir and the Fire Nyfiyir are recited every day,
when one finds oneself in the proximity of those elements. The
Fire Ny&yij is recited with the Pendm on (see Vend. p. 168, 7).

The first four Nyiyi^ must be recited especially on the days over
which the Izads invoked preside; that is to say, on the KhdrshS^,
Mihir, Mdh, and Abfin days (the eleventh, sixteenth, twelfth, and
tenth days of the month) K
